<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove Talagrand's conjecture that integral and fractional expectation thresholds are within a universal constant factor, with the same covering budget.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): For every finite nonempty $X$ and every nonempty proper increasing family $\mathcal F\subseteq2^X$, $$q_f(\mathcal F)\le25\cdot512^4\,q(\mathcal F).$$ Both thresholds use the budget $1/2$. The constant is independent of $\|X\|$, the sizes of the minimal members of $\mathcal F$, and the support of the fractional cover.
  - Explanation: Compares fractional and integral expectation thresholds with a universal factor and an unchanged covering budget.
- **C2** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-auto-remark-1)): For $p>0$, the weak-smallness probability weights $\beta_S$ in [[Talagrand2010](#ref-Talagrand2010), Definition 6.1] give fractional coefficients $\beta_S/(2p^{\|S\|})$; capping these at one preserves coverage and does not increase cost. Conversely, the masses $2g(S)p^{\|S\|}$ can be completed to a probability distribution by adding the deficit at the empty set. Passing from an arbitrary class to its upward closure preserves both cover constraints. Thus the increasing-family formulation of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> also resolves Conjecture 6.3 in its original form.
  - Explanation: Relates the original weak-smallness formulation to the increasing-family covering formulation.
- **C3** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-multiscale-selector)): Let $X$ be a finite set, let $\mathcal F$ be a nonempty family of nonempty subsets of $X$, and suppose that $\mathcal F$ is not $p$-small, where $0<p\le 1$. For each $H\in\mathcal F$, let $\lambda_H$ be a probability mass function on $X$ supported on $H$. Set $D=256$, and let $s\ge 0$ be an integer such that $$p\sum_{i=1}^s D^i\le \frac12.$$ Independently color the elements of $X$ by a map $a:X\to\{1,\ldots,s+1\}$, with color probabilities $$\label{sel:color-probabilities} \pi_i=D^ip\quad(1\le i\le s), \qquad \pi_{s+1}=1-p\sum_{i=1}^sD^i.$$ For a coloring $v$, write $A_i(v)=\{x\in X:v(x)\le i\}$. With probability at least $9/10$, there is an $H\in\mathcal F$ such that $$\label{sel:simultaneous-capture} \lambda_H(A_i(a))\ge 1-2^{-i} \qquad(1\le i\le s).$$ In particular, with probability at least $9/10$, there is an $H\in\mathcal F$ for which $$\label{sel:average-color} \sum_{x\in X}a(x)\lambda_H(x)\le 2.$$
  - Explanation: Finds a common member whose mass is captured simultaneously at all scales, hence has bounded mean color.
- **C4** (theorem, [source](#stmt-sel-truncation)): Let $\lambda$ be a probability mass function on a finite set $X$, let $A\subseteq X$, and let $m\ge 0$ and $0<d<1$. Call $u\in[0,1]$ admissible if $$\label{sel:admissible-cutoff} \{x:\lambda(x)>u\}\subseteq A, \qquad \sum_{x\in X}\min\{\lambda(x),u\} \bigl(\mathbf 1_A(x)-(1-d)\bigr)\ge mu.$$ If there is an admissible cutoff, there is a largest one, $\varepsilon$, and it satisfies $$\|\{x:\lambda(x)>\varepsilon\}\|\le \frac{m}{d}.$$
  - Explanation: A maximal admissible truncation cutoff bounds the number of strictly heavier points.
- **C5** (theorem, [source](#stmt-cor-two-unions)): Let $X$ be a finite nonempty set, let $0<p<1$, and let $\mathcal A\subseteq2^X$ satisfy $\mu_p(\mathcal A)>1/2$. Set $C=25\cdot512^4$. There is a family $\mathcal G\subseteq2^X$ such that $$\operatorname{Bad}_2(\mathcal A)\subseteq\langle\mathcal G\rangle, \qquad \sum_{S\in\mathcal G}\left(\frac{p}{2C}\right)^{\|S\|}\le\frac12.$$ In particular, $\operatorname{Bad}_2(\mathcal A)$ is $(p/(2C))$-small. No monotonicity assumption on $\mathcal A$ is needed.
  - Explanation: Combines the comparison with the cited fractional discrete-convexity input to cover the two-union obstruction family.

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Containment closure, integral cost, fractional closure and fractional cost** ([source](#pm-h-2)): For a family $\mathcal G\subseteq2^X$ and a function $g:2^X\to[0,1]$, define $$\langle\mathcal G\rangle =\{H\subseteq X:\exists S\in\mathcal G,\ S\subseteq H\}, \qquad c_p(\mathcal G)=\sum_{S\in\mathcal G}p^{\|S\|},$$ and $$\langle g\rangle =\left\{H\subseteq X:\sum_{S\subseteq H}g(S)\ge1\right\}, \qquad w(g,p)=\sum_{S\subseteq X}g(S)p^{\|S\|}.$$
- **p-smallness** ([source](#pm-h-2)): We say that $\mathcal F$ is *$p$-small* if there is a family $\mathcal G$ with $\mathcal F\subseteq\langle\mathcal G\rangle$ and $c_p(\mathcal G)\le1/2$.
- **Integral and fractional expectation thresholds** ([source](#pm-h-2)): Its integral and fractional expectation thresholds are $$\begin{aligned} q(\mathcal F) &=\max\{p\in[0,1]:\mathcal F\text{ is }p\text{-small}\},\\ q_f(\mathcal F) &=\max\{p\in[0,1]:\exists g:2^X\to[0,1], \mathcal F\subseteq\langle g\rangle, \ w(g,p)\le1/2\}. \end{aligned}$$
- **Parameter and empty-generator convention** ([source](#pm-h-2)): Throughout, $p\in[0,1]$ and $p^0=1$, including when $p=0$.
- **Cumulative color sets** ([source](#stmt-thm-multiscale-selector)): For a coloring $v$, write $A_i(v)=\{x\in X:v(x)\le i\}$.
- **Admissible cutoff** ([source](#stmt-sel-truncation)): Call $u\in[0,1]$ admissible if $$\label{sel:admissible-cutoff} \{x:\lambda(x)>u\}\subseteq A, \qquad \sum_{x\in X}\min\{\lambda(x),u\} \bigl(\mathbf 1_A(x)-(1-d)\bigr)\ge mu.$$
- **Normalized member mass** ([source](#round:mass)): For each $H\in\mathcal F$, define $$\label{round:mass} M_H=\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq H}g(S) \ge1-g(\varnothing)\ge\frac12, \qquad \lambda_H(x)=\frac1{M_H} \sum_{\substack{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq H\\x\in S}} \frac{g(S)}{\|S\|}\quad(x\in X).$$
- **Two-union obstruction family** ([source](#sec:consequence)): For $\mathcal A\subseteq2^X$, write $\mu_p(\mathcal A)=\mathbb P(X_p\in\mathcal A)$ and define $$\operatorname{Bad}_2(\mathcal A) =\{S\subseteq X:S\nsubseteq A_1\cup A_2 \text{ for all }A_1,A_2\in\mathcal A\}.$$
- **Exponential vertex variables and weighted product sum** ([source](#round:scale)): Denote this event by $\mathcal E$, and set $$\label{round:variables} Y_x=B^{4-a(x)},\qquad Z=\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq X}g(S)\prod_{x\in S}Y_x.$$
- **Random-set threshold** ([source](#pm-h-3)): Let $p_c(\mathcal F)$ denote the unique parameter for which $\mathbb P(X_p\in\mathcal F)=1/2$.

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-sel-truncation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sel-truncation): Compactness of admissible cutoffs and the right-hand piecewise-linear slope. -> Heavy-point cardinality bound
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-thm-multiscale-selector) / [proof](#sel:feasibility): Finite minimization of total color-level movement. -> Changed-vertex sets covering the family
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-thm-multiscale-selector) / [proof](#sel:encoding-bound): Common witness reconstruction, encoding counts, weighted AM–GM and a geometric sum. -> Selector failure probability bound
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#round:upper): Contradiction between selector-event and independent-product expectation bounds. -> Integral cover existence at the scaled parameter
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-cor-two-unions) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-two-unions): Apply the cited fractional discrete-convexity input and the threshold comparison. -> Small integral cover of the two-union obstruction family

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I1**: The main comparison preserves the covering budget and uses a universal factor independent of ground-set size, minimal member sizes and fractional support.
- **I2**: If an admissible cutoff exists, its largest value has at most m/d masses strictly larger than it.
- **I3**: The selector captures one common member at all scales; separate witnesses per scale do not imply the claimed average-color bound.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A1**: Apply the main comparison to arbitrary nonmonotone configurations without constructing an increasing family. -> The main theorem hypotheses are not established.
- **A2**: Use any admissible cutoff to claim the heavy-set bound. -> Admissibility alone does not establish the maximal-cutoff bound.
- **A3**: Combine per-scale selector witnesses into one average-color certificate. -> Different members at different scales do not produce one bounded-mean member.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. Lemma 4 gives a finite constructive primitive: the maximal admissible cutoff for a finite rational probability mass function can be found from finitely many piecewise-linear breakpoints and roots. This implements that lemma only, not a general efficient fractional-cover rounding algorithm or a proof of Theorem 1.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON list of cases {weights:[rational strings], A:[indices], m:rational, d:rational}.
# PRECONDITIONS: 1<=n<=6, weights>=0 summing to 1, A unique valid indices, m>=0, 0<d<1; at most 5000 cases.
# OUTPUTS: JSON list: null if no admissible cutoff, else {epsilon: exact rational string, heavy: strict heavy count}.
# TIME: O(k*n^2) exact rational operations for k cases; bit cost depends on rational input size.
# SPACE: O(k*n) rational storage including input/output; O(n) working storage per case.
# ARITHMETIC: python-flint fmpq exact rational arithmetic; no floating point.
# FAILURE: invalid input raises ValueError with nonzero exit; no-admissible case is null.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpq

def cutoff(case):
    w = [fmpq(x) for x in case['weights']]
    a = set(case['A'])
    m, d = fmpq(case['m']), fmpq(case['d'])
    n = len(w)
    if not (1 <= n <= 6 and len(a) == len(case['A']) and all(type(i) is int and 0 <= i < n for i in a) and all(x >= 0 for x in w) and sum(w) == 1 and m >= 0 and 0 < d < 1):
        raise ValueError('invalid preconditions')
    def excess(u):
        return sum(min(x, u) * (d if i in a else d-1) for i, x in enumerate(w)) - m*u
    floor = max([w[i] for i in range(n) if i not in a] + [fmpq(0)])
    cuts = sorted(set([floor, fmpq(1)] + [x for x in w if x >= floor]))
    feasible = [u for u in cuts if excess(u) >= 0]
    for left, right in zip(cuts, cuts[1:]):
        fl, fr = excess(left), excess(right)
        if fl >= 0 and fr < 0:
            feasible.append(left + (right-left)*fl/(fl-fr))
    if not feasible:
        return None
    epsilon = max(feasible)
    heavy = sum(x > epsilon for x in w)
    return {'epsilon': str(epsilon), 'heavy': heavy}

cases = json.load(sys.stdin)
if not isinstance(cases, list) or len(cases) > 5000:
    raise ValueError('case bound')
print(json.dumps([cutoff(case) for case in cases], separators=(',', ':')))
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: the paper gives no concrete software-system mapping. Its cover-existence theorem can constrain a separately proved finite monotone configuration model, but supplies no program-correctness, latency, memory, or reliability contract. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: no game or simulation application is stated. Independent subset selection is a mathematical assumption; interacting agents, sequential play, and correlated simulation events require additional models. |
| tools | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Conditional adaptation to an exact finite cover-analysis tool: once its input is proved to encode the source containment-cost model, the theorem supplies a mathematical comparison against which results can be checked. The paper provides no tool implementation or performance guarantee. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Lemma 4 supplies a finite truncation primitive: on rational input satisfying the source hypotheses, admissibility is piecewise linear in the cutoff, allowing an inferred exact breakpoint-and-interval search for the largest admissible cutoff. The paper states the mathematical property, not executable code or a complexity bound. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: no representation, update operation, or data-structure bound is established. Finite subset families are mathematical inputs rather than a storage design. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: no accelerator architecture, parallel kernel, throughput, or resource bound is provided. The dimension-independent constant is a mathematical threshold factor. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: no adversary model, hardness assumption, or cryptographic reduction appears. Cover costs under independent sampling cannot certify security against adaptive or correlated attacks. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: no learning loss, generalization, convergence, or trained-model guarantee is derived. A separately proved finite monotone feature model would support only the source cover comparison. |
| optimisation_operations_research | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Conditional mathematical contract for the source weighted containment-cover problem: a feasible fractional certificate at parameter r implies an integral certificate with the same source budget at r/(25\cdot512^4). This is a parameter comparison for costs p^{\|S\|}, not a fixed-cost objective approximation or an efficient rounding algorithm. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: no distributed protocol, consistency rule, fault tolerance, or communication bound is proved. Independent random vertex coloring does not establish independent distributed failures. |
| other (finite_probabilistic_combinatorics) | True | strong | adaptable | stated | Direct mathematical applicability to finite probabilistic combinatorics: Theorem 1 compares integral and fractional expectation thresholds. Corollary 5 applies to the increasing family Bad_2(A) for a class A of source measure greater than the source budget, even when A is nonmonotone. No engineering implementation claim is added. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L1** (hypothesis): The main comparison requires a finite nonempty ground set and a nonempty proper increasing family. An arbitrary class can be treated through upward closure only when the resulting family meets these hypotheses. Blocked use: ['Direct application to infinite sets, empty or improper families, or a nonmonotone property without checking its upward closure.']
- **L2** (hypothesis): Containment-event costs in the paper use independent element inclusion with a common parameter. Dependent or heterogeneous activation models require a separate reduction. Blocked use: ['Replacing generator costs by correlated inclusion probabilities or unequal per-element probabilities without proof.']
- **L3** (hypothesis): Both expectation thresholds use the same stated covering budget. Changing the budget is outside the stated comparison. Blocked use: ['Transferring the constant to a different cost budget without a new argument.']
- **L4** (approximation): The theorem provides a universal threshold comparison, rather than equality of the two thresholds or rounding at the original fractional parameter. Blocked use: ['Claiming zero-loss rounding, an optimal constant, or unchanged activation parameter.']
- **L5** (edge_case): The zero-parameter convention retains unit cost for the empty generator. The proof handles the zero parameter separately using a cover without the empty set. Blocked use: ['Treating the empty generator as free at zero or dividing by a zero fractional parameter.']
- **L6** (hypothesis): The selector theorem permits a nonincreasing family, but requires nonempty members, supported probability masses, failure of smallness, and the geometric scale-budget condition. Blocked use: ['Applying selector success to a small family, an empty member, unnormalised vertex weights, or unrestricted scale depth.']
- **L7** (hypothesis): Selector capture at different scales must hold for one common family member. Separate witnesses per scale do not supply the mean-color conclusion. Blocked use: ['Combining separately successful scale tests whose witnesses differ.']
- **L8** (hypothesis): The maximal-cutoff lemma is conditional on admissibility existing. Its input is a probability mass function, with nonnegative charge and a deficit strictly between zero and one. Blocked use: ['Returning a certified cutoff for infeasible inputs, arbitrary signed weights, negative charge, or endpoint deficit values.']
- **L9** (edge_case): Heavy elements are defined by a strict inequality above the largest cutoff. Equal-cutoff weights remain saturated during a rightward perturbation. Blocked use: ['Replacing the heavy-set predicate by a non-strict comparison, especially at the unit cutoff.']
- **L10** (nonconstructive): The finite proof chooses minimizers over colorings and family members with fixed tie orders. It does not state an efficient general rounding algorithm; a finite cutoff routine implements only the truncation primitive. Blocked use: ['Claiming polynomial-time fractional-to-integral rounding or using a cutoff selftest as verification of the entire threshold theorem.']
- **L11** (edge_case): The two-union corollary needs an open-interval parameter and strict measure excess over the budget; monotonicity of the original class is unnecessary. An empty bad family has the empty cover. Blocked use: ['Applying the corollary at equality of the measure premise or imposing monotonicity on the original class as a necessary condition.']
- **L12** (counterexample_scope): The paper compares combinatorial covering thresholds. Engineering reliability, latency, security, numerical stability and operational correctness require an explicit model and independent arguments. Blocked use: ['Using the abstract threshold comparison as an unconditional guarantee for a software system.']
- **L13** (formal_scope): Independent verification of generated blocks and a formal proof certificate for the full repaired theorem are ABSENT from this lane audit. Finite primitive tests cannot substitute for either. Blocked use: ['Marking generated verification fields as passed based on this limits file or a finite primitive selftest.']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 175 (`lean/docs/175.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [Integral and fractional expectation thresholds are equivalent](../../preprints/Integral-and-fractional-expectation-thresholds-are-equivalent-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [Talagrand's discrete-convexity conjecture](../../preprints/Talagrands-discrete-convexity-conjecture-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> Talagrand's expectation-threshold conjecture asks whether the fractional and integral expectation thresholds are comparable by an absolute constant. The formalized result proves $q_f(\mathcal F)\le25\cdot512^4 q(\mathcal F)$ for every nonempty proper increasing family on a finite nonempty ground set. Both thresholds use cover budget $1/2$, and fractional covers may assign weights to every subset, including the empty set.
>
> The formalized result proves Talagrand's discrete-convexity assertion with $k=2^{75}$. If a family of subsets of a finite nonempty ground set has Bernoulli-$p$ measure at least $1-1/k$, then the sets not contained in a union of $k$ members form a $p$-small family: they have a containment cover of total $p$-cost at most $1/2$. This holds for every $0<p<1$, with repeated members allowed in the union and no monotonicity assumption.

```lean
import Mathlib.Data.Fintype.Powerset
import Mathlib.Data.Finset.Union
import Mathlib.Basic.Real.Basic
import Mathlib.Algebra.Order.Archimedean.Real.Basic
import Mathlib.Algebra.Order.BigOperators.Group.Finset
import Mathlib.Algebra.BigOperators.Group.Finset.Piecewise

namespace OAI

namespace TalagrandThreshold

open scoped BigOperators

abbrev Family (α : Type*) := Finset (Finset α)

variable {α : Type*} [Fintype α] [DecidableEq α]

noncomputable section

def cost (p : ℝ) (G : Family α) : ℝ := ∑ S ∈ G, p ^ S.card

def Covers (G F : Family α) : Prop := ∀ H ∈ F, ∃ S ∈ G, S ⊆ H

def Small (p : ℝ) (F : Family α) : Prop :=
  ∃ G : Family α, Covers G F ∧ cost p G ≤ 1 / 2

def fracCost (p : ℝ) (g : Finset α → ℝ) : ℝ :=
  ∑ S : Finset α, g S * p ^ S.card

def FractionalCover (F : Family α) (g : Finset α → ℝ) : Prop :=
  (∀ S, 0 ≤ g S ∧ g S ≤ 1) ∧
    ∀ H ∈ F, 1 ≤ ∑ S ∈ H.powerset, g S

def FractionalSmall (p : ℝ) (F : Family α) : Prop :=
  ∃ g : Finset α → ℝ, FractionalCover F g ∧ fracCost p g ≤ 1 / 2

def Increasing (F : Family α) : Prop :=
  ∀ H ∈ F, ∀ K : Finset α, H ⊆ K → K ∈ F

def integralFeasible (F : Family α) : Set ℝ :=
  {p | 0 ≤ p ∧ p ≤ 1 ∧ Small p F}

def fractionalFeasible (F : Family α) : Set ℝ :=
  {p | 0 ≤ p ∧ p ≤ 1 ∧ FractionalSmall p F}

def q (F : Family α) : ℝ := sSup (integralFeasible F)

def qf (F : Family α) : ℝ := sSup (fractionalFeasible F)

end

theorem talagrand_expectation_threshold_equivalence [Nonempty α]
    (F : Family α) (_hF : F.Nonempty) (hproper : F ≠ Finset.univ)
    (hIncreasing : Increasing F) :
    qf F ≤ ((25 : ℝ) * (512 : ℝ) ^ 4) * q F := by
  sorry

end TalagrandThreshold

end OAI
```

If a family of subsets of a finite nonempty ground set has Bernoulli-$p$ measure at least $1-1/k$, then the sets not contained in a union of $k$ members form a $p$-small family: they have a containment cover of total $p$-cost at most $1/2$. This holds for every $0<p<1$, with repeated members allowed in the union and no monotonicity assumption."
```json
  },
  "status": "present",
  "toolchain": "leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1"
}
```
<!-- PM:END pm.formal.v2 -->

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove Talagrand's conjecture that integral and fractional expectation thresholds are within a universal constant factor, with the same covering budget.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

| anchor | kind | label | number | first sentence |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| [stmt-thm-main](#stmt-thm-main) | theorem | `thm:main` | 1 | For every finite nonempty $X$ and every nonempty proper increasing family $\mathcal F\subseteq2^X$, $$q_f(\mathcal F)\le25\cdot512^4\,q(\mathcal F).$$ Both thresholds use the budget $1/2$. |
| [stmt-auto-remark-1](#stmt-auto-remark-1) | remark | - | - | Remark 2* (Talagrand’s original formulation). |
| [stmt-thm-multiscale-selector](#stmt-thm-multiscale-selector) | theorem | `thm:multiscale-selector` | 3 | Let $X$ be a finite set, let $\mathcal F$ be a nonempty family of nonempty subsets of $X$, and suppose that $\mathcal F$ is not $p$-small, where |
| [stmt-sel-truncation](#stmt-sel-truncation) | lemma | `sel:truncation` | 4 | Let $\lambda$ be a probability mass function on a finite set $X$, let $A\subseteq X$, and let $m\ge 0$ and $0<d<1$. |
| [stmt-cor-two-unions](#stmt-cor-two-unions) | corollary | `cor:two-unions` | 5 | Let $X$ be a finite nonempty set, let $0<p<1$, and let $\mathcal A\subseteq2^X$ satisfy $\mu_p(\mathcal A)>1/2$. |

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

Expectation thresholds measure how cheaply an increasing family can be covered by elementary containment events. Their fractional relaxation allows a member to be covered by a total weight of one, distributed among its subsets. Talagrand conjectured that this relaxation changes the expectation threshold by at most a universal factor <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Talagrand2010](#ref-Talagrand2010), Conjecture 6.3]. We prove this conjecture.

Let $X$ be a finite nonempty set and let $\mathcal F$ be a nonempty proper increasing subfamily of $2^X$. For a family $\mathcal G\subseteq2^X$ and a function $g:2^X\to[0,1]$, define $$\langle\mathcal G\rangle
 =\{H\subseteq X:\exists S\in\mathcal G,\ S\subseteq H\},
 \qquad
 c_p(\mathcal G)=\sum_{S\in\mathcal G}p^{|S|},$$ and $$\langle g\rangle
 =\left\{H\subseteq X:\sum_{S\subseteq H}g(S)\ge1\right\},
 \qquad
 w(g,p)=\sum_{S\subseteq X}g(S)p^{|S|}.$$ Throughout, $p\in[0,1]$ and $p^0=1$, including when $p=0$. Thus a cover supplies, inside each member of $\mathcal F$, at least one generator $S\in\mathcal G$. If $X_p$ includes the elements of $X$ independently with probability $p$, the cost of that generator is $\mathbb P(S\subseteq X_p)=p^{|S|}$. We say that $\mathcal F$ is *$p$-small* if there is a family $\mathcal G$ with $\mathcal F\subseteq\langle\mathcal G\rangle$ and $c_p(\mathcal G)\le1/2$. Its integral and fractional expectation thresholds are $$\begin{aligned}
 q(\mathcal F)
 &=\max\{p\in[0,1]:\mathcal F\text{ is }p\text{-small}\},\\
 q_f(\mathcal F)
 &=\max\{p\in[0,1]:\exists g:2^X\to[0,1],
       \mathcal F\subseteq\langle g\rangle,
       \ w(g,p)\le1/2\}.
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, $q(\mathcal F)\le q_f(\mathcal F)$, by taking $g$ to be the indicator of an integral cover.

These maxima are well-defined. Since $\varnothing\notin\mathcal F$, both feasible parameter sets contain zero. Integral feasibility is a finite union of closed subsets of $[0,1]$. Fractional feasibility is the projection of the compact set of pairs $(p,g)$ satisfying the displayed constraints in $[0,1]\times[0,1]^{2^X}$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every finite nonempty $X$ and every nonempty proper increasing family $\mathcal F\subseteq2^X$, $$q_f(\mathcal F)\le25\cdot512^4\,q(\mathcal F).$$ Both thresholds use the budget $1/2$. The constant is independent of $|X|$, the sizes of the minimal members of $\mathcal F$, and the support of the fractional cover.*

</div>

The conclusion preserves the covering budget: every fractional cover of cost at most $1/2$ at a parameter $r$ admits an integral cover of cost at most $1/2$ at $r/(25\cdot512^4)$.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Thresholds, selectors, and earlier comparisons

Expectation thresholds arose in the study of when a random set first has a prescribed increasing property. Let $p_c(\mathcal F)$ denote the unique parameter for which $\mathbb P(X_p\in\mathcal F)=1/2$. A union bound applied to an integral cover gives $q(\mathcal F)\le p_c(\mathcal F)$; taking expectations in a fractional cover gives $q_f(\mathcal F)\le p_c(\mathcal F)$ as well. Kahn and Kalai <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[KahnKalai2007](#ref-KahnKalai2007)] conjectured the upper bound $p_c(\mathcal F)=O(q(\mathcal F)\log(2|X|))$. Frankston, Kahn, Narayanan, and Park <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[FKNP2021](#ref-FKNP2021)], building on the sunflower methods of Alweiss, Lovett, Wu, and Zhang <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[ALWZ2021](#ref-ALWZ2021)], proved the fractional bound $$p_c(\mathcal F)=O(q_f(\mathcal F)\log\ell),
 \qquad
 \ell=\max\{2,\max\{|H|:H\text{ is a minimal member of }\mathcal F\}\}.$$ Park and Pham <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[ParkPham2024threshold](#ref-ParkPham2024threshold)] proved the corresponding bound with $q$ in place of $q_f$, resolving the Kahn–Kalai conjecture. These theorems estimate the random-set threshold. Talagrand’s comparison asks whether fractional certificates themselves admit integral replacements at a constant loss.

Early results obtained such replacements by exploiting the structure of the sets with positive fractional weight. Talagrand settled the case of singleton sets <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Talagrand2010](#ref-Talagrand2010), Section 6]; DeMarco and Kahn <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[DeMarcoKahn2015](#ref-DeMarcoKahn2015)] treated clique-counting certificates, and Frankston, Kahn, and Park <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[FrankstonKahnPark2022](#ref-FrankstonKahnPark2022)] treated arbitrary weights on pairs. Fischer and Person extended these methods to nearly linear uniform hypergraphs <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[FischerPerson2025](#ref-FischerPerson2025)] and further clique cases <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[FischerPerson2025further](#ref-FischerPerson2025further)]. They also proved the comparison with high probability for constant weights on random uniform supports <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[FischerPerson2026random](#ref-FischerPerson2026random)].

Selector processes supply a different route: they seek a member whose assigned vertex mass is substantially captured by a random subset. Park and Pham proved Talagrand’s selector-process conjecture <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[ParkPham2024selector](#ref-ParkPham2024selector)], and Bednorz, Martynek, and Meller <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[BMM2022](#ref-BMM2022)] gave a proof using truncated weights. Dubroff, Kahn, and Park <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[DKP2024](#ref-DKP2024), Theorem 1.2] used selector estimates to round any fractional cover whose positive coefficients occur only on sets of cardinality at most $t$, with a loss depending on $t$. Pham sharpened this loss to $O(\log(2t))$ <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Pham2024](#ref-Pham2024), arXiv version 1, Theorem 1.2]. Here $t$ bounds the cardinalities of the weighted sets, not their number or the sizes of the minimal members of $\mathcal F$. For example, for $1\le k\le|X|$, the family $\{H\subseteq X:|H|\ge k\}$ has the fractional cover $g(\{x\})=1/k$, zero elsewhere: its weighted sets are singletons, while its minimal members have cardinality $k$.

Fischer and Person’s sampling argument for sufficiently large sets <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[FischerPerson2025](#ref-FischerPerson2025), Theorem 10], combined with Pham’s logarithmic rounding bound, gives a loss of order $\log\log(4|X|)$. This consequence is recorded in the expanded version of Pham’s paper <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Pham2026](#ref-Pham2026), Corollary 1.8]. More recently, Park <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Park2026](#ref-Park2026), Theorem 1.2] obtained the dimension-independent bound $$q_f(\mathcal F)\le Kq(\mathcal F)\max\{1,\log\log(1/q(\mathcal F))\}$$ for a universal $K$. The factor here still grows as $q(\mathcal F)$ tends to zero. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> removes both kinds of dependence. Section <a href="#sec:consequence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequence">4</a> applies it to Li’s fractional discrete-convexity theorem <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Li2026](#ref-Li2026)]: for any class $\mathcal A\subseteq2^X$ with $\mathbb P(X_p\in\mathcal A)>1/2$, the sets not contained in a union of two members of $\mathcal A$ have a small integral cover at a universal constant multiple of $p$.

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 2* (Talagrand’s original formulation). For $p>0$, the weak-smallness probability weights $\beta_S$ in <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Talagrand2010](#ref-Talagrand2010), Definition 6.1] give fractional coefficients $\beta_S/(2p^{|S|})$; capping these at one preserves coverage and does not increase cost. Conversely, the masses $2g(S)p^{|S|}$ can be completed to a probability distribution by adding the deficit at the empty set. Passing from an arbitrary class to its upward closure preserves both cover constraints. Thus the increasing-family formulation of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> also resolves Conjecture 6.3 in its original form.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The selector estimate and the rounding mechanism

Our selector estimate controls one member simultaneously at several scales. Assign to each $H\in\mathcal F$ a probability mass $\lambda_H$ supported on $H$. Independently color the vertices by integers $a(x)\in\{1,\ldots,s+1\}$, and let $A_i=\{x:a(x)\le i\}$. Theorem <a href="#thm:multiscale-selector" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:multiscale-selector">3</a> specifies geometrically increasing color probabilities for which failure of $p$-smallness forces, with probability at least $9/10$, a single $H$ satisfying $$\lambda_H(A_i)\ge1-2^{-i}\quad(1\le i\le s).$$ The uncaptured masses are summable, so this member has bounded mean color: $$\sum_x a(x)\lambda_H(x)
 =1+\sum_{i=1}^s\bigl(1-\lambda_H(A_i)\bigr)\le2.$$ Separate estimates at individual scales could choose different members and would not give this bound for any one member.

To apply the estimate to a fractional cover of cost at most $1/2$ at parameter $r$, distribute each nonempty weight $g(S)$ equally among the vertices of $S$ and normalize on each member, following Pham <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Pham2024](#ref-Pham2024), arXiv version 1, Section 3]. The mean vertex color then equals the weighted mean of the average colors of those sets. Thus a fixed positive amount of fractional weight lies on sets with average color at most four. For a suitable fixed base $B>1$, the independent variables $Y_x=B^{4-a(x)}$ satisfy $\prod_{x\in S}Y_x\ge1$ on all these sets. Their common mean can at the same time be made a constant multiple of $p$ by a suitable choice of the number of colors. Taking $p$ to be a sufficiently small constant multiple of $r$, independence bounds the expectation of $$Z=\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq X}g(S)\prod_{x\in S}Y_x$$ from above using the fractional covering cost, while the selector event bounds it from below. Section <a href="#sec:rounding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:rounding">3</a> makes these bounds incompatible. Only nonemptiness of the weighted sets enters this final comparison, so their cardinalities need no upper bound.

The proof of the selector estimate in Section <a href="#sec:selector" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:selector">2</a> develops the minimum-fragment method of Park and Pham <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[ParkPham2024selector](#ref-ParkPham2024selector)] and Pham’s towers of fragments <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Pham2024](#ref-Pham2024), arXiv version 1, Section 2], together with the maximal truncations of Bednorz, Martynek, and Meller <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[BMM2022](#ref-BMM2022), Section 3.1]. For each coloring and each member, we move vertices to earlier colors until a set of truncated inequalities holds, minimizing the total number of color levels moved. The resulting changed sets form a cover. To estimate its cost, we record the final coloring and the numbers of vertices making each possible color change. These data determine one common member satisfying the truncated inequalities for the entire group of original colorings. Maximal truncation bounds the number of candidate vertices at each color, and a weighted AM–GM estimate pays for their allocation among the scales. Keeping all color changes in the same record is what permits the simultaneous estimate.

The random vertex colors used below are independent. We write $\lambda_H(A)=\sum_{x\in A}\lambda_H(x)$. Empty sums are zero and empty products are one.

<a id="sec:selector"></a>
### A multiscale selector estimate

The key estimate finds a single member of the family whose mass is captured at every scale. The family in this section need not be increasing. Recall that a family $\mathcal F\subseteq 2^X$ is *$p$-small* if there is a family $\mathcal G\subseteq 2^X$ such that every $H\in\mathcal F$ contains some $G\in\mathcal G$ and $\sum_{G\in\mathcal G}p^{|G|}\le 1/2$.

The argument combines the minimum-fragment method of Park and Pham <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[ParkPham2024selector](#ref-ParkPham2024selector), Definitions 2.3 and 2.5, Proposition 2.6] and its multiscale development by Pham <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Pham2024](#ref-Pham2024), arXiv version 1, Section 2] with the maximal-cutoff argument of Bednorz, Martynek, and Meller <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[BMM2022](#ref-BMM2022), Lemma 3.6]. All the estimates needed here are proved below.

<a id="stmt-thm-multiscale-selector"></a>
<div id="thm:multiscale-selector" class="theorem">

<a id="sel:simultaneous-capture"></a>
<a id="sel:average-color"></a>
**Theorem 3** (Multiscale selector estimate). *Let $X$ be a finite set, let $\mathcal F$ be a nonempty family of nonempty subsets of $X$, and suppose that $\mathcal F$ is not $p$-small, where $0<p\le 1$. For each $H\in\mathcal F$, let $\lambda_H$ be a probability mass function on $X$ supported on $H$. Set $D=256$, and let $s\ge 0$ be an integer such that $$p\sum_{i=1}^s D^i\le \frac12.$$ Independently color the elements of $X$ by a map $a:X\to\{1,\ldots,s+1\}$, with color probabilities <a id="sel:color-probabilities"></a>$$\label{sel:color-probabilities}
  \pi_i=D^ip\quad(1\le i\le s),
  \qquad
  \pi_{s+1}=1-p\sum_{i=1}^sD^i.$$ For a coloring $v$, write $A_i(v)=\{x\in X:v(x)\le i\}$. With probability at least $9/10$, there is an $H\in\mathcal F$ such that $$\label{sel:simultaneous-capture}
  \lambda_H(A_i(a))\ge 1-2^{-i}
  \qquad(1\le i\le s).$$ In particular, with probability at least $9/10$, there is an $H\in\mathcal F$ for which $$\label{sel:average-color}
  \sum_{x\in X}a(x)\lambda_H(x)\le 2.$$*

</div>

For colorings where simultaneous capture fails, the proof will construct covers from sets of vertices moved to earlier colors. We will sum these costs against the probabilities of the failing colorings. The estimate comes from locating the changed vertices within small sets determined by the final coloring and the numbers moved between colors. The following truncation observation supplies the size bound for these sets.

<a id="stmt-sel-truncation"></a>
<div id="sel:truncation" class="lemma">

<a id="sel:admissible-cutoff"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Maximal truncation). *Let $\lambda$ be a probability mass function on a finite set $X$, let $A\subseteq X$, and let $m\ge 0$ and $0<d<1$. Call $u\in[0,1]$ admissible if $$\label{sel:admissible-cutoff}
  \{x:\lambda(x)>u\}\subseteq A,
  \qquad
  \sum_{x\in X}\min\{\lambda(x),u\}
       \bigl(\mathbf 1_A(x)-(1-d)\bigr)\ge mu.$$ If there is an admissible cutoff, there is a largest one, $\varepsilon$, and it satisfies $$|\{x:\lambda(x)>\varepsilon\}|\le \frac{m}{d}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-sel-truncation"><summary>Proof of stmt-sel-truncation</summary>

*Proof.* The inclusion in <a href="#sel:admissible-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:admissible-cutoff">[sel:admissible-cutoff]</a> is equivalent to $$u\ge \max\bigl(\{\lambda(x):x\notin A\}\cup\{0\}\bigr).$$ The remaining inequality is closed in $u$, since its two sides are continuous. Thus the nonempty set of admissible cutoffs is compact and has a largest element $\varepsilon$.

Put $R=\{x:\lambda(x)>\varepsilon\}$. If $\varepsilon=1$, then $R=\varnothing$ and the assertion holds. Otherwise, consider $$f(u)=\sum_{x\in X}\min\{\lambda(x),u\}
          \bigl(\mathbf 1_A(x)-(1-d)\bigr)-mu.$$ On a sufficiently short interval to the right of $\varepsilon$, the only unsaturated terms are those indexed by $R$. Each of these points belongs to $A$, so the slope on that interval is $d|R|-m$. This statement also holds when $\varepsilon=0$ or when $\varepsilon$ equals one of the weights: weights equal to the cutoff remain constant when the cutoff increases. An increase of the cutoff preserves the inclusion in <a href="#sel:admissible-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:admissible-cutoff">[sel:admissible-cutoff]</a>. If $d|R|-m$ were positive, it would also preserve $f(u)\ge 0$ for a small increase, contradicting maximality. Hence $d|R|\le m$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-sel-truncation-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-sel-truncation</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:multiscale-selector" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:multiscale-selector">3</a>.* If $s=0$, the only color is $1$, and both conclusions are immediate. Assume henceforth that $s\ge 1$, and put $d_i=2^{-i}$ for $1\le i\le s$. Call a coloring $a$ *bad* if no $H\in\mathcal F$ satisfies <a href="#sel:simultaneous-capture" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:simultaneous-capture">[sel:simultaneous-capture]</a>. We will prove $\mathbb P(a\text{ is bad})\le 1/10$.

<a id="sel:feasibility"></a>
*Feasible moves and a cover.* We may move vertices to earlier colors, obtaining a coloring $z\le a$ pointwise. Write $$U(a,z)=\{x:z(x)<a(x)\},
  \qquad
  m_i(a,z)=|A_i(z)\setminus A_i(a)|.$$ Call such a move *feasible* if there are $H\in\mathcal F$ and $\varepsilon_1,\ldots,\varepsilon_s\in[0,1]$ such that, for every $i$, $$\label{sel:feasibility}
\begin{split}
  R_i:=\{x:\lambda_H(x)>\varepsilon_i\}&\subseteq A_i(z),\\
  \sum_{x\in X}\min\{\lambda_H(x),\varepsilon_i\}
       \bigl(\mathbf 1_{A_i(z)}(x)-(1-d_i)\bigr)
       &\ge m_i(a,z)\varepsilon_i.
\end{split}$$ The right side charges $\varepsilon_i$ for each vertex in $A_i(z)\setminus A_i(a)$. Removing one of these vertices lowers the truncated sum by at most $\varepsilon_i$ while reducing that charge by exactly $\varepsilon_i$; hence the inequality survives. If the vertex has mass at most $\varepsilon_i$, it lies outside $R_i$, so the inclusion survives as well. We will apply this balance when delaying a vertex from color $i$ to color $i+1$.

If the unchanged move $z=a$ is feasible, then $a$ is not bad. Indeed, in this case $m_i(a,a)=0$. Replacing each truncated weight by its original value adds only nonnegative terms to the left side of <a href="#sel:feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:feasibility">[sel:feasibility]</a>: any excess weight lies in $R_i\subseteq A_i(a)$ and has coefficient $d_i$. The resulting inequality is $\lambda_H(A_i(a))-(1-d_i)\ge0$, for the same $H$ at every $i$.

Fix orders on the finite sets of colorings and members of $\mathcal F$. For every $a$ and $H_0\in\mathcal F$, choose a feasible coloring $z$ with $U(a,z)\subseteq H_0$ minimizing $$L(a,z)=\sum_{x\in X}(a(x)-z(x)),$$ breaking ties by the fixed order. This minimum exists: the move that sets $z(x)=1$ on $H_0$ and leaves other colors unchanged is feasible with $H=H_0$ and all cutoffs zero. Notice that the witness $H$ in the minimization is otherwise unrestricted; it need not equal $H_0$.

<a id="sel:cover-cost"></a>
Let $\mathcal Z(a)$ be the set of chosen colorings, with repetitions removed. The changed sets $U(a,z)$, $z\in\mathcal Z(a)$, cover $\mathcal F$, because each $H_0$ contains its chosen changed set. Failure of $p$-smallness therefore implies $$\sum_{z\in\mathcal Z(a)}p^{|U(a,z)|}>\frac12.$$ This remains true if distinct colorings have the same changed set: the displayed sum is then at least the cost of the cover with its repeated sets removed. Define $P(v)=\prod_{x\in X}\pi_{v(x)}$ for every coloring $v$. It follows that $$\label{sel:cover-cost}
  \frac12\mathbb P(a\text{ is bad})
  \le
  \sum_{a\text{ bad}}P(a)
       \sum_{z\in\mathcal Z(a)}p^{|U(a,z)|}.$$ Every changed set appearing on the right is nonempty, since a bad coloring has no feasible unchanged move.

*A witness determined by the final coloring and the counts.* Group the pairs $(a,z)$ on the right of <a href="#sel:cover-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:cover-cost">[sel:cover-cost]</a> by $z$ and the array of counts $$n_{ih}=|\{x:z(x)=i,\ a(x)=h\}|
  \qquad(1\le i<h\le s+1).$$ We call this array the *profile*. Put $$t_i=\sum_{h>i}n_{ih},
  \qquad t=\sum_{i=1}^s t_i=|U(a,z)|.$$ The profile also determines every quantity in the feasibility tests that depends on $a$, because <a id="sel:profile-crossings"></a>$$\label{sel:profile-crossings}
  m_i(a,z)=\sum_{j\le i<h}n_{jh}.$$

<a id="sel:large-weight-bound"></a>
Fix $z$ and a profile occurring in the sum. Choose the first $H\in\mathcal F$ that can witness <a href="#sel:feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:feasibility">[sel:feasibility]</a> with these data. Such a witness exists, and this choice uses only $z$ and the profile. For this fixed $H$, choose at every $i$ the largest admissible cutoff in <a href="#sel:feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:feasibility">[sel:feasibility]</a>. The choices for the different indices impose no restrictions on one another. Lemma <a href="#sel:truncation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sel:truncation">4</a> gives $$\label{sel:large-weight-bound}
  |R_i|\le \frac{m_i(a,z)}{d_i}\le 2^i t.$$ The resulting $H$, cutoffs, and sets $R_i$ are fixed for the entire group. In particular, they witness feasibility for every original coloring $a$ in that group, irrespective of the member $H_0$ that led to the choice of $z$.

<a id="sel:changed-vertices"></a>
For each such original coloring we claim that $$\label{sel:changed-vertices}
  \{x:z(x)=i<a(x)\}\subseteq R_i
  \qquad(1\le i\le s).$$ Suppose instead that a vertex $x$ on the left lies outside $R_i$. Delay its color in $z$ from $i$ to $i+1$, obtaining $z'$. Since $a(x)>i$, we still have $z'\le a$. Choose an $H_0$ for which $z$ was a minimum. The changed set of $z'$ is contained in that of $z$, so it is still contained in $H_0$.

Only $A_i(z)$ changes under this delay: it loses $x$, while all other $A_j(z)$ remain unchanged. Correspondingly, $m_i(a,z)$ decreases by one and all other $m_j(a,z)$ remain unchanged. The inclusion in test $i$ stays true because $x\notin R_i$. The left side of that test decreases by $\min\{\lambda_H(x),\varepsilon_i\}\le\varepsilon_i$, whereas its right side decreases by exactly $\varepsilon_i$. Thus the same $H$ and cutoffs witness feasibility of $z'$. But $L(a,z')=L(a,z)-1$, contradicting the defining minimum. This proves <a href="#sel:changed-vertices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:changed-vertices">[sel:changed-vertices]</a> for every $a$ in the group. Figure <a href="#fig:one-level-delay" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:one-level-delay">1</a> illustrates why moving one vertex back by one level changes only one of the feasibility tests.

<figure id="fig:one-level-delay">

<figcaption>The one-level delay in the reconstruction argument. A vertex originally colored $h > i$ has been moved to $i$; delaying it to $i + 1$ removes it from $A_{i}$ alone. At the same time $m_{i}$ decreases by one, lowering the right side of <a href="#sel:feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:feasibility">[sel:feasibility]</a> by $ε_{i}$. Its membership in every other cumulative set $A_{j}$ stays fixed. The drawing is schematic; $h$ may equal $i + 1$.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-one-level-delay"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:one-level-delay`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1.2cm,y=1cm,>=Latex,font=\small]
\draw[->,gray] (0,0) -- (7,0) node[right]{color};
\foreach \x/\lab in {1/{1},3/{i},4/{i+1},6/{h}} {
  \draw (\x,-.08) -- (\x,.08);
  \node[below] at (\x,-.1) {$\lab$};
}
\fill[blue!55!black] (3,0) circle (2pt);
\fill[blue!55!black] (6,0) circle (2pt);
\draw[->,thick,blue!55!black] (5.95,.15) to[bend right=30]
 node[above] {chosen move} (3.05,.15);
\draw[->,thick] (3,-.7) -- node[below]{delay one level} (4,-.7);
\draw[dashed,gray] (3.5,-1.2) -- (3.5,.55);
\node[anchor=east] at (3.3,-1.5) {inside $A_i$};
\node[anchor=west] at (3.7,-1.5) {outside $A_i$};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 7 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A horizontal axis labeled "color" with an arrow pointing to the right. On the axis, four points are marked with tick marks labeled from left to right: "1", "$i$", "$i + 1$", and "$h$". A curved arrow above the axis arcs from "$h$" leftward to "$i$" and is labeled "chosen move". An arrow below the axis points from "$i$" rightward to "$i + 1$" and is labeled "delay one level". A vertical dashed line crosses the axis between "$i$" and "$i + 1$"; below the axis, the region to the left of the dashed line is labeled "inside $A_i$", and the region to the right is labeled "outside $A_i$".
```


<a id="sel:encoding-bound"></a>
*Counting the original colorings.* Given $z$ and the profile, an original coloring $a$ is determined by its changed vertices of each color $i$ in $z$, together with their original colors $h>i$. By <a href="#sel:changed-vertices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:changed-vertices">[sel:changed-vertices]</a>, the number of possibilities is at most $$\label{sel:encoding-bound}
  \prod_{i=1}^s
    \binom{|R_i|}{t_i}
    \binom{t_i}{(n_{ih})_{h>i}},$$ where the second factor is a multinomial coefficient. The sets $R_i$ may overlap and may contain vertices of other colors in $z$. Counting arbitrary subsets of them only adds possibilities to <a href="#sel:encoding-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:encoding-bound">[sel:encoding-bound]</a>; every actual coloring has a unique encoding of the indicated form.

<a id="sel:entropy-bound"></a>
For $t>0$, <a href="#sel:large-weight-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:large-weight-bound">[sel:large-weight-bound]</a> and $\binom{n}{k}\le(en/k)^k$ give $$\label{sel:entropy-bound}
  \prod_{i=1}^s\binom{|R_i|}{t_i}
  \le
  \prod_{i:t_i>0}\left(\frac{e\,2^i t}{t_i}\right)^{t_i}
  \le \prod_{i=1}^s(e\,4^i)^{t_i}.$$ To see the last inequality, apply weighted AM–GM with weights $\alpha_i=t_i/t$ on the positive rows: $$\prod_{i:t_i>0}
     \left(\frac{2^{-i}}{\alpha_i}\right)^{\alpha_i}
  \le \sum_{i:t_i>0}2^{-i}\le1.$$ Raising this inequality to the power $t$ yields exactly the second inequality in <a href="#sel:entropy-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:entropy-bound">[sel:entropy-bound]</a>. Rows with $t_i=0$ contribute a factor of one.

<a id="sel:likelihood"></a>
<a id="sel:profile-contribution"></a>
For each pair in the group, its probability and cost satisfy the exact identity $$\label{sel:likelihood}
  p^tP(a)=P(z)
      \prod_{i=1}^s\left(\frac p{\pi_i}\right)^{t_i}
      \prod_{i<h}\pi_h^{n_{ih}}.$$ All color probabilities are positive. Combining <a href="#sel:encoding-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:encoding-bound">[sel:encoding-bound]</a>–<a href="#sel:likelihood" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:likelihood">[sel:likelihood]</a>, the contribution of a fixed $z$ and profile to the right side of <a href="#sel:cover-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:cover-cost">[sel:cover-cost]</a> is at most $$\label{sel:profile-contribution}
  P(z)\prod_{i=1}^s
  \left[
     \left(\frac{e\,4^i p}{\pi_i}\right)^{t_i}
     \binom{t_i}{(n_{ih})_{h>i}}
     \prod_{h>i}\pi_h^{n_{ih}}
  \right].$$ The witness and the sets $R_i$ can depend on the full profile, but none of that dependence remains in this bound.

<a id="sel:tuple-sum"></a>
For fixed row sums $t_1,\ldots,t_s$, we can consequently sum <a href="#sel:profile-contribution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:profile-contribution">[sel:profile-contribution]</a> over all nonnegative integer profiles with those row sums, including profiles that do not occur. The multinomial theorem gives, for each row, $$\sum_{\substack{(n_{ih})_{h>i}\ge0\\
                   \sum_{h>i}n_{ih}=t_i}}
      \binom{t_i}{(n_{ih})_{h>i}}
      \prod_{h>i}\pi_h^{n_{ih}}
  =\left(\sum_{h>i}\pi_h\right)^{t_i}\le1.$$ Finally, summing over all colorings $z$ uses $\sum_zP(z)=1$. Thus the right side of <a href="#sel:cover-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:cover-cost">[sel:cover-cost]</a> is at most $$\label{sel:tuple-sum}
  \sum_{\substack{t_1,\ldots,t_s\ge0\\
                   t_1+\cdots+t_s>0}}
       \prod_{i=1}^s
         \left(\frac{e\,4^i p}{\pi_i}\right)^{t_i}
  =
  \sum_{\substack{t_1,\ldots,t_s\ge0\\
                   t_1+\cdots+t_s>0}}
       \prod_{i=1}^s\left(\frac e{64^i}\right)^{t_i},$$ where both sums run over integer tuples. Put $\rho=\sum_{i=1}^s e/64^i$. For a fixed total $\ell=t_1+\cdots+t_s$, each monomial in <a href="#sel:tuple-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:tuple-sum">[sel:tuple-sum]</a> occurs in the expansion of $\rho^\ell$ with a multinomial coefficient at least one. Since $$\rho<\frac e{63}<\frac1{21},$$ we obtain $$\text{right side of \eqref{sel:cover-cost}}
  \le\sum_{\ell\ge1}\rho^\ell
  =\frac{\rho}{1-\rho}<\frac1{20}.$$ Together with <a href="#sel:cover-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:cover-cost">[sel:cover-cost]</a>, this proves that the bad probability is at most $1/10$.

For every nonbad coloring the same $H$ satisfies all inequalities in <a href="#sel:simultaneous-capture" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:simultaneous-capture">[sel:simultaneous-capture]</a>. Since the colors are integers, $$\sum_{x\in X}a(x)\lambda_H(x)
  =1+\sum_{i=1}^s\bigl(1-\lambda_H(A_i(a))\bigr)
  \le1+\sum_{i=1}^s2^{-i}\le2.$$ This proves <a href="#sel:average-color" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:average-color">[sel:average-color]</a> and completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:rounding"></a>
### Rounding a fractional cover

We convert the color bound into independent numerical variables on the vertices. Their means will be small, while the common member supplied by Theorem <a href="#thm:multiscale-selector" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:multiscale-selector">3</a> will force a positive amount of fractional-cover mass onto sets whose products are at least one. Comparing the resulting lower and upper bounds on the same expectation completes the proof. This conversion follows the selector-to-rounding approach of Dubroff, Kahn, and Park <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[DKP2024](#ref-DKP2024), Section 2] and Pham <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Pham2024](#ref-Pham2024), arXiv version 1, Section 3]. In particular, the equal distribution of a set’s weight among its vertices in <a href="#round:mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="round:mass">[round:mass]</a> is Pham’s construction. The simultaneous color bound supplies the additional control used here for arbitrary set sizes.

<details id="proof-stmt-sel-truncation-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-sel-truncation</summary>

<a id="round:cover"></a>
*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Put $$D=256,\qquad B=512,\qquad C=25B^4.$$ Let $r\in(0,1]$ and suppose that $g:2^X\to[0,1]$ satisfies $$\label{round:cover}
 \sum_{S\subseteq H}g(S)\ge1\quad(H\in\mathcal F),
 \qquad
 \sum_{S\subseteq X}g(S)r^{|S|}\le\frac12.$$ Set $p=r/C$. We prove that $\mathcal F$ is $p$-small.

<a id="round:mass"></a>
First observe that $g(\varnothing)\le1/2$, since its contribution to the second sum in <a href="#round:cover" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="round:cover">[round:cover]</a> is $g(\varnothing)$. For each $H\in\mathcal F$, define $$\label{round:mass}
 M_H=\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq H}g(S)
 \ge1-g(\varnothing)\ge\frac12,
 \qquad
 \lambda_H(x)=\frac1{M_H}
 \sum_{\substack{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq H\\x\in S}}
 \frac{g(S)}{|S|}\quad(x\in X).$$ The function $\lambda_H$ vanishes off $H$ and is a probability mass function: summing over $x$ counts each nonempty $S$ exactly $|S|$ times.

Suppose, for a contradiction, that $\mathcal F$ is not $p$-small. Let $s$ be the largest nonnegative integer for which <a id="round:scale"></a>$$\label{round:scale}
 p\sum_{i=1}^sD^i\le\frac12.$$ Such an integer exists: zero satisfies the restriction, and $p>0$ makes the geometric sums unbounded. Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:multiscale-selector" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:multiscale-selector">3</a> to these probability masses and this choice of $s$. Thus the colors $a(x)$ are independent, with $$\mathbb P(a(x)=i)=\pi_i=D^ip\quad(1\le i\le s),
 \qquad
 \mathbb P(a(x)=s+1)=\pi_{s+1}=1-p\sum_{i=1}^sD^i.$$ With probability at least $9/10$, there is an $H\in\mathcal F$ such that $\sum_x a(x)\lambda_H(x)\le2$. Denote this event by $\mathcal E$, and set <a id="round:variables"></a>$$\label{round:variables}
 Y_x=B^{4-a(x)},\qquad
 Z=\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq X}g(S)\prod_{x\in S}Y_x.$$

<a id="round:lower"></a>
Fix an outcome in $\mathcal E$ and a corresponding member $H$. Writing $\overline a(S)=|S|^{-1}\sum_{x\in S}a(x)$ for nonempty $S$, we have $$\frac1{M_H}\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq H}
 g(S)\overline a(S)
 =\sum_x a(x)\lambda_H(x)\le2.$$ Apply Markov’s inequality to the probability distribution $g(S)/M_H$ on the nonempty subsets of $H$. It gives $$\sum_{\substack{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq H\\
                  \overline a(S)\le4}}g(S)\ge\frac{M_H}{2}.$$ For every set in this sum, $$\prod_{x\in S}Y_x
 =B^{\,|S|(4-\overline a(S))}\ge1.$$ Consequently $Z\ge M_H/2\ge1/4$ on $\mathcal E$. This holds for each outcome, although the corresponding $H$ may depend on the coloring. Since $Z$ is nonnegative everywhere, $$\label{round:lower}
 \mathbb EZ\ge\frac14\mathbb P(\mathcal E)\ge\frac9{40}.$$

<a id="round:upper"></a>
For the upper bound, maximality of $s$ gives $$\frac12<p\sum_{i=1}^{s+1}D^i\le2pD^{s+1}.$$ As $B\ge D$, it follows that <a id="round:tail"></a>$$\label{round:tail}
 B^{-(s+1)}\le D^{-(s+1)}<4p.$$ The variables $Y_x$ are independent and identically distributed, and their common mean $\mu$ satisfies <a id="round:mean"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mu
 &=B^4\left(p\sum_{i=1}^s(D/B)^i
            +\pi_{s+1}B^{-(s+1)}\right)\notag\\
 &\le B^4\left(p\sum_{i=1}^s2^{-i}+4p\right)
 \le5B^4p=\frac r5.\label{round:mean}
\end{aligned}$$ The same estimates hold when $s=0$, with the first sums empty. Independence now yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb EZ
 &=\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq X}g(S)\mu^{|S|}\notag\\
 &\le\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq X}g(S)(r/5)^{|S|}
 \le\frac15\sum_{\varnothing\ne S\subseteq X}g(S)r^{|S|}
 \le\frac1{10}.\label{round:upper}
\end{aligned}$$ Here the factor $1/5$ is valid because every set in the sum is nonempty. The bounds <a href="#round:lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="round:lower">[round:lower]</a> and <a href="#round:upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="round:upper">[round:upper]</a> contradict one another.

Thus $\mathcal F$ is $r/C$-small for every admissible $r>0$. At $r=0$, the family $\mathcal F$ itself is a cover of cost zero, since $\varnothing\notin\mathcal F$. Taking $r=q_f(\mathcal F)$, with this last observation covering the case $q_f(\mathcal F)=0$, proves $$q_f(\mathcal F)\le25\cdot512^4\,q(\mathcal F).$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:consequence"></a>
### A consequence for unions of two sets

The comparison also strengthens a recent bound for sets that cannot be covered by two members of a large class. For $\mathcal A\subseteq2^X$, write $\mu_p(\mathcal A)=\mathbb P(X_p\in\mathcal A)$ and define $$\operatorname{Bad}_2(\mathcal A)
 =\{S\subseteq X:S\nsubseteq A_1\cup A_2
                   \text{ for all }A_1,A_2\in\mathcal A\}.$$ When $\mu_p(\mathcal A)>1/2$, Li’s fractional discrete-convexity theorem <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Li2026](#ref-Li2026), Corollary 3.1] provides a fractional cover for this family. Combining that input with Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> gives the following integral conclusion.

<a id="stmt-cor-two-unions"></a>
<div id="cor:two-unions" class="corollary">

**Corollary 5**. *Let $X$ be a finite nonempty set, let $0<p<1$, and let $\mathcal A\subseteq2^X$ satisfy $\mu_p(\mathcal A)>1/2$. Set $C=25\cdot512^4$. There is a family $\mathcal G\subseteq2^X$ such that $$\operatorname{Bad}_2(\mathcal A)\subseteq\langle\mathcal G\rangle,
 \qquad
 \sum_{S\in\mathcal G}\left(\frac{p}{2C}\right)^{|S|}\le\frac12.$$ In particular, $\operatorname{Bad}_2(\mathcal A)$ is $(p/(2C))$-small. No monotonicity assumption on $\mathcal A$ is needed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-two-unions"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-two-unions</summary>

*Proof.* If $\operatorname{Bad}_2(\mathcal A)$ is empty, take $\mathcal G=\varnothing$. Otherwise this is a nonempty increasing family. It is proper because $\mathcal A$ is nonempty and hence $\varnothing\notin\operatorname{Bad}_2(\mathcal A)$. Li’s theorem, in the covering formulation given by Park <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Park2026](#ref-Park2026), Theorem 3.4], supplies a fractional cover of $\operatorname{Bad}_2(\mathcal A)$ of cost at most $1/2$ at parameter $p/2$. Its coefficients may be capped at one without affecting coverage or increasing cost. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> therefore yields an integral cover of cost at most $1/2$ at parameter $p/(2C)$. ◻

</details>

Park <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Park2026](#ref-Park2026), Corollary 3.5] obtained the same conclusion with smallness parameter $$\frac{p}{K\max\{1,\log\log(1/p)\}}$$ for a universal $K$. Corollary <a href="#cor:two-unions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:two-unions">5</a> removes the double logarithmic loss while retaining unions of two members and the covering budget $1/2$.

```json
      "caption": "The one-level delay in the reconstruction argument. A vertex originally colored $h > i$ has been moved to $i$; delaying it to $i + 1$ removes it from $A_{i}$ alone. The drawing is schematic; $h$ may equal $i + 1$.",

          "exact_quote": "The one-level delay in the reconstruction argument. A vertex\noriginally colored $h>i$ has been moved to $i$; delaying it to $i+1$\nremoves it from $A_i$ alone. At the same time $m_i$ decreases by one,\nlowering the right side of \\eqref{sel:fe",

      "witness_description": "A horizontal axis labeled \"color\" with an arrow pointing to the right. On the axis, four points are marked with tick marks labeled from left to right: \"1\", \"$i$\", \"$i + 1$\", and \"$h$\".
```
